11: 
12: # Does a model-centred clock carry information beyond optimizer telemetry? Closure report of Pilot 2
13: 
14: Ioan Daniel Ionici, independent researcher, Oradea, Romania. ORCID 0009-0009-8183-3656.
15: 
16: Version 0.1 (draft), November 2026. Code, preregistrations and logs: [DOI-ARCHIVE]; https://github.com/danionici-creator/pilot2-model-clock-audit
17: 
18: ## Abstract
19: 
20: The Perspective paper *A Time of Their Own* (v1.3) left open whether an agent's accumulated internal change, an "experiential index" c_e, carries information beyond the statistics an optimizer logs anyway. Pilot 2 set out to test this, under rules preregistered locally (hashed before each run, not deposited), on a synthetic continual-learning world and on a small language model trained with Adam and the replay schedule of FOREVER, a recent method that triggers replay with a "model-centric" clock τ = Σ‖Δθ‖. The confirmatory test was never reached. In our implementations, every candidate tested (three parameter-space distances, a functional distance, and τ itself) was reconstructed from cheap telemetry or from same-cost competitors with held-out R² ≥ 0.90 (exceptions only when a schedule family was left out of training), which makes the planned hypothesis unidentifiable under the protocol's own gate. Driving FOREVER's replay with the free clock Ση‖m̂‖ (learning rate times the norm of Adam's first moment) instead of τ left retention within the preregistered margins in our port, where the two clocks triggered replay at practically the same steps, and in aggregate with the authors' own code (three pairs of runs, after reproducing their reported score within 0.53 points in one run on one task order). There, Ση‖m̂‖ triggers replay systematically later (by 0–36 of 313 steps); a 5.3-point difference on one task did not recur consistently from common starting points (−0.5 and +3.3 points). The line "own time as accumulated internal change" therefore ends here without having been tested: its cheap candidates are not identifiable, and τ showed no scheduling advantage in the regimes tested. This is not evidence that they carry no information about forgetting.
21: 
22: ## 1. The question and the outcome in brief
23: 
24: **Question.** Perspective v1.3 (Ionici 2026c, Level 2) defines an experiential index c_e(t) = Σ_{τ<t} D(S(τ), S(τ+1)), the accumulated change of an agent's own state under a distance D, read against an external clock, and states that whether it "carries information beyond logged optimizer statistics … remains open". Candidates range "from cheap and weak to costly and valid". Version 1.2 had said of the proposed tests that "the author does not plan to pursue them"; Pilot 2 was undertaken afterwards as a change of plan (version 1.3 says only that "the tests are offered to others"), and this report closes it.
25: 
26: **Outcome.**
27: 1. *Reconstruction (identifiability).* In every setting of our own implementations (synthetic world, LM port, SuperNI port), the rate of each candidate clock is predicted out of sample from cheap online telemetry, or from a competitor computed from the same forward passes, with R² ≥ 0.90 (§5.1; the exceptions, all under extrapolation to a schedule family left out of training, are named there). Under the protocol's gate G2 this makes the confirmatory hypothesis H1 (c_e predicts forgetting beyond telemetry) not identifiable, so H1 was never run and the forgetting targets were never opened.
28: 2. *Scheduling.* Triggering FOREVER's replay with Ση‖m̂‖, with the learning-rate schedule alone, or with the step count gave retention within the preregistered margins of the τ-driven schedule in our port; with the authors' code the aggregate differences stayed within a descriptive ±2-point margin, and the comparison of single tasks is inconclusive (§5.2).
29: 3. *The exception, and what remains of it.* With the authors' code the two clocks schedule replay differently: Ση‖m̂‖ triggers later than τ, systematically (in 16 of 17 comparable triggers of two pairs, once at the same step, never earlier; up to 36 of 313 steps). In the first pair of runs the first task differed by 5.3 points. Repeated from common starting points (preregistered 2 × 2 design, clock × task-0 adapter), the difference had opposite signs in the two pairs (−0.51 and +3.29 points); under the rule fixed beforehand it is **not reproduced robustly** and cannot be attributed to the clock. The reconstruction R² on tasks with replay, 0.75 in the first pair (post hoc), was 0.97 in the repeated run (§5.1, §5.2).
30: 
31: What ends here, *in this framework*, is the empirical line "own time = accumulated internal change" as a source of information beyond telemetry: its cheap candidates cannot be separated from telemetry, so the line has no testable candidate left in these regimes. This is not a refutation of H1, which was never run. The framework: small models (a 0.93 M-parameter Transformer; Qwen3-0.6B with LoRA), synthetic facts, five SuperNI tasks and four classification tasks. Nothing here concerns large models, full fine-tuning or subjective experience.
32: 
33: ## 2. Background
34: 
35: **FOREVER** (Feng et al. 2026, ACL 2026, arXiv:2601.03938; code github.com/WoodScene/FOREVER, commit bbeb549) defines model-centric time τ = Σ_t ‖θ_t − θ_{t−1}‖₂ over the LoRA factors, reset per task. A "model day" is the value of τ after the first 24 steps of a task; replay from a small memory buffer is triggered when τ crosses d × day, d ∈ {1, 2, 4, 7, 15, 30, 60, 90, 120} (the paper writes {1, 2, 4, 7, 15, 30, …}; the full list is in the code), and the replay strength β is set from the same update norms. The paper's only ablation of the time signal is against a step-based calibration (+1.2 points of overall performance and +1.1 BWT; Table 2, one benchmark: SuperNI, task order 7), and it reports that τ_day does not correlate with final forgetting across tasks (r = 0.2182, p = 0.4536; Appendix C).
36: 
37: **Known mechanism.** For Adam-type optimizers each coordinate moves by η m̂_i/(√v̂_i + ε), plus the decoupled weight decay where it is used (none in our language-model port). Hence ‖Δθ‖ ≈ η‖m̂‖/√v̄, where √v̄ stands for a typical value of √v̂_i; the approximation holds when v̂ varies little across coordinates and over time, and the per-coordinate preconditioning is what can break it (Kosson, Messmer & Jaggi 2024; Liu et al. 2025, who report that AdamW's update RMS "is usually around 0.2 to 0.4"; see also Wang & Aitchison 2025). A close relation between τ and the free quantity Ση‖m̂‖ is therefore expected; the question was whether the remainder carries information that matters. No published audit of the FOREVER clock was found: Semantic Scholar listed 13 citing papers on 4 Nov 2026 and 13 on 9 Nov, and none examines the clock (checked on 4 Nov, and again on 9 Nov from the abstracts of the seven most recent).
38: 
39: **Other uses of accumulated change.** FOREVER is the method tested here, but the question concerns a class of methods that read the accumulated change of a model as a clock, a control signal or a measure of importance.
40: - Path-based learning-rate schedules compare the cumulative path of the parameters with that of a random walk; for Adam, the authors find that the optimizer's internal preconditioning makes this comparison inconsistent unless it is corrected (Atamna et al. 2025).
41: - Synaptic Intelligence accumulates, for each parameter, the contribution of its updates along the trajectory (Zenke, Poole & Ganguli 2017). Benzing (2022) showed that this importance, like that of MAS, approximates the square root of the Fisher information, a statistic of the gradients, and that for SI the relation, "and in fact its performance", is due to a previously unknown bias. This is the same pattern as here, at the level of single parameters.
42: - Work on the magnitude of updates as a cause of forgetting studies a quantity that, under Adam, the optimizer sets (He, Bai & Yang 2026).
43: - Schedulers of repetition decide when to review training items from estimated forgetting delays (Amiri, Miller & Savova 2017), or which stored samples to replay from the interference of the next update (Aljundi et al. 2019).
44: 
45: The results of §5 bear on these classes to different degrees. On path-based clocks they bear most directly, since under Adam the path length is to first order a sum the optimizer already keeps. On per-parameter importance they extend a published pattern without testing it. On schedulers of repetition they suggest including the schedule-only clock and the step count as baselines, and no more: those methods also choose what to repeat, and the regimes tested here were narrow. Costlier quantities integrated along the trajectory, such as the influence of training examples traced through training (Pruthi et al. 2020), lie outside what was tested.
46: 
47: **Pilot 1** (Ionici 2026a, b) found that an apparent advantage of binding episodes to a summary of internal change disappeared once the control model was made competent; the signal was carried by local update statistics. Pilot 2 asked the same question of the clock itself.
48: 
49: ## 3. How the plan changed (an honest timeline)
50: 
51: The study did not follow one frozen protocol. The order of events matters for reading the results.
52: 
53: | Date (2026) | Step | Record |
54: |---|---|---|
55: | 27 Oct – 4 Nov | Protocol drafts v0.1–v0.4: confirmatory H1 with gate G2; Stage A (prediction of forgetting), Stage B (scheduling) | `Versiuni_anterioare/`, `Pilot2_Protocol_EN_draft.md` (v0.4) |
56: | 27 Oct – 4 Nov | AI evaluations of drafts v0.1–v0.4, each verified point by point (for v0.4, six points judged fatal after verification); direction opinions from five AI systems on 4 Nov | `Recenzii/` (informative annex; see its README) |
57: | 4 Nov | Stage 0 technical rounds: **G2 preview fails for all candidates** (R² 0.91–0.99) | `Stadiul0/Stadiul0_note_lucru.md` §9 |
58: | 4–6 Nov | LM arm (Qwen3-0.6B + LoRA, FOREVER schedule ported): τ rate reconstructed (0.94–0.96) | dossier of 7 Nov, R5–R9 |
59: | 6 Nov | Colloquium of five AI systems under a decision rule fixed in advance: **direction C** (close the parameter-clock line; one decisive test per open exception), 4 votes to 1 | `Colocviu/Regula_de_decizie.md`, `Verificare_raspunsuri_2026-11-06.md` |
60: | 6–7 Nov | Decisive tests T-A, T-B (preregistered), T-C, T-D (criteria fixed before computing) | `Stadiul0/preinregistrare_TA_TB_2026-11-06.md`, `lm/tc_criterii.md`, `td_criterii.md` |
61: | 7 Nov | SuperNI check S1, S2 (preregistered) | `Stadiul0/preinregistrare_SuperNI_2026-11-07.md` |
62: | 7–8 Nov | Reproduction with the authors' code, R1 (τ) and R2 (η‖m̂‖) (preregistered + one addendum) | `Stadiul0/forever_repro/preinregistrare_FOREVER_repro_2026-11-07.md` |
63: | 8 Nov | Short colloquium on the R2 per-task difference: analysis (d) + stage 1 of repeated runs, 3 votes of 3 | `Colocviu/Regula_de_decizie_2026-11-08.md`, `Verificare_raspunsuri_2026-11-08.md` |
64: | 8–9 Nov | Stage 1 of repeated runs (preregistered, v3): not reproduced robustly (D_A1 −0.51, D_B1 +3.29); stage 2 not needed for the verdict, not run | `Stadiul0/forever_repro/preinregistrare_FOREVER_repetari_2026-11-08.md`, `runs/forever_repetari_verdict.json` |
65: | 9–10 Nov | Final review round of this report and of Perspective v1.4 (two automated reviewers, then six AI systems; adoption rule fixed in advance); corrections applied; post hoc checks (a) evaluation noise, (b) T-A retention of the facts held in memory, (c) skipped steps | `Recenzii/Runda_finala_2026-11-09/`, `runs/forever_eval_noise_posthoc.json`, `runs/ta_memory_posthoc.json`, `runs/forever_skip_check_posthoc.log` |
66: 
67: Two consequences. First, **the confirmatory study was never run**: the closure rests on identifiability (G2) and on scheduling comparisons, not on H1. The forgetting columns of the synthetic world (T1) were never opened. Second, **preregistration here means local**: each preregistration, with the code and analysis scripts it names, was hashed (SHA-256) before the runs it governs, and the hash lists are in the archive; but none was deposited publicly before running, so the timing is attested only by the author's own records (§7). The tests after 4 Nov form an adaptive sequence: each was designed after the previous results, and its rules were fixed and hashed before its own runs (T-C and T-D: before computing, on runs that already existed). The preregistration of stage 1 went through three versions on 8 Nov (13:43; 13:58, after verified remarks of one AI reviewer; and 14:30, after the short colloquium, which replaced the decision rule), all before the first run of stage 1 (15:08).
68: 
69: ## 4. Set-ups
70: 
71: **Synthetic world (CPU).** Pre-LN decoder-only Transformer, 4 layers, d = 128, 4 heads, vocabulary 512, context 4 tokens, 925,440 parameters; explicit AdamW (β 0.9/0.999, weight decay 0.01, clip 1.0, η 3 × 10⁻⁴), bit-identical to `torch.optim.AdamW`. Forty target tasks of 256 arbitrary key → value facts and a reference battery R of 256 items; a "childhood" of 3,072 steps, then a "life" of 20,480 steps under one of six schedule families (F1 round-robin rehearsal; F2 interleaved re-exposures with varying batch sizes; F3 cosine learning-rate restarts; F4 idle pauses; F5 a mixture; F6 distractor segments with label noise). Candidates for D: D1, the Euclidean norm of the parameter update; D1_rel, the per-layer relative update; D2, the update weighted by a lagged second moment; D3, the functional change (mean √JS divergence of outputs on R, over 8-step chunks). Each candidate is read as a rate: its increment over an interval divided by ΔN, the number of optimizer steps in the interval. Telemetry ("B_strong"): steps, tokens, Ση, Σ loss, gradient and Adam-moment norms, weight-decay path, parameter norms, local update statistics, loss changes on R; the features labelled C6 are those that contain D1's own statistics and those of the Adam step at the ends of the interval. Same-cost competitors of D3 from the same forward passes: C10c (mean |change of the per-item loss| on R), C10p (mean |Δ p(target)|), C10h (mean |Δ output entropy|). Runs are bit-identical on repetition.
72: 
73: **Language-model arm, port (GPU, RTX 3050 Laptop, 4 GB).** Qwen3-0.6B (Yang et al. 2025) with LoRA (Hu et al. 2021; r 8, α 16, q_proj and v_proj; 1,146,880 trainable parameters), the FOREVER schedule re-implemented from their code; synthetic facts "code ABC-DEF is WORD" (31 answer words), 10 tasks of 512 facts each, 60 epochs on task 0 and 10 on the others, memory of 16 facts per earlier task; per-step telemetry (η, loss, gradient norms, GradScaler skips, ‖m̂‖, √Tr v̂, the τ increment). Declared deviations from FOREVER: one batch of 32 instead of 4 × 8 accumulation, loss on the answer token, one data stream across a task's phases, bf16 in T-A. Runs are bit-identical on repetition on the GPU. In every arm the replay strength β is computed from ‖Δθ‖, as in FOREVER; only the clock that triggers replay changes. Each phase starts a new AdamW, learning-rate schedule and (in fp16) GradScaler, as in the authors' code.
74: 
75: **SuperNI (port).** The first five tasks of FOREVER's order 7 of Super-NaturalInstructions (Wang et al. 2022): task1572, task363, task1290, task181, task002; with their Qwen script settings (micro-batch 4 × 8, up to 512 tokens, 2 memory epochs, 2% memory per earlier task) and declared deviations (bf16; 3 / 10 epochs instead of 10 / 60; no length grouping; memory rule corrected for generation tasks).
76: 
77: **Authors' code.** FOREVER at commit bbeb549 with their library versions (torch 2.8.0+cu128, transformers 4.57.0) in a separate environment, on the same laptop GPU (RTX 3050 Laptop, 4 GB, Windows 11). The authors ran their experiments on 8 NVIDIA H20 GPUs (Appendix H). Their scripts assign one GPU to each job (`CUDA_VISIBLE_DEVICES`, `torchrun --nproc_per_node=1`), so a single run does not depend on several GPUs. The code itself names no hardware. We used their arguments for Qwen3-0.6B, the "Standard CL" sequence dbpedia → amazon → yahoo → agnews (classification datasets of Zhang, Zhao & LeCun 2015; `dataset_id` 4 in their repository = order 1 of the paper, Appendix G, Table 14), seed 42 as fixed in their code, their evaluation scripts unchanged. The adaptations, all documented and hashed: local model-cache path; test-data path in the two generators (hard-coded to the SuperNI folder); `python` instead of `torchrun` on one GPU, with a UTF-8 console and Hugging Face offline mode; the training script's actual file name (§8, note 3); and, for tasks 1–3, the PyTorch allocator without caching (`PYTORCH_NO_CUDA_MEMORY_CACHING=1`; the 4 GB card ran out of memory otherwise; arithmetic unchanged). The clock-swap variant changes only the counter that triggers replay; β is still computed from ‖Δθ‖.
78: 
79: ## 5. Results
80: 
81: ### 5.1 Reconstruction of the clocks (gate G2)
82: 
83: **Synthetic world, technical rounds** (pre-fixed as blind to forgetting; models untuned). Held-out R² of the rate of each candidate, test on each tech3 run, training on the other runs (technical rounds of the synthetic world: tech2 and tech3 on 4 Nov; tech4 on 6 Nov, with the corrected code):
84: 
85: | D | B_strong, OLS | B_strong, GBDT | ΔN only | ΔN + Ση‖m̂‖ |
86: |---|---|---|---|---|
87: | D1 | 0.98–0.99 | 0.98–0.99 | −0.06–0.33 | 0.94 |
88: | D1_rel | 0.96–0.98 | 0.97 | −0.11–0.30 | 0.92 |
89: | D2 | 0.99 | 0.99 | −0.06–0.27 | 0.99 |
90: | D3 | 0.92–0.96 | 0.91–0.95 | −0.13–0.22 | 0.85 |
91: 
92: Source: `Stadiul0/g2_preview.py`; `Stadiul0_note_lucru.md` §9. The rate varies substantially (coefficient of variation 0.37–0.73), so the schedule families do decouple steps from change; steps alone explain little. These rounds (4 Nov) ran with an earlier configuration and code than the one described in §4, before the corrections of 6 Nov. Among the differences: a context of 8 tokens instead of 4, and in tech2 also rule tasks and a different reference battery. The resolved configuration of each run is in its `meta.json`; the code of that day was not kept (§7). The tech4 round and T-B, run with the corrected code, give the same picture (below).
93: 
94: **T-D, audit of the reconstruction** (criteria fixed before computing). (1) Without the features that contain D1's own statistics (C6), the result is unchanged (e.g. D1 0.983–0.994 OLS). (2) With the mechanism of the test family left out of training, the one-scalar model ΔN + Ση‖m̂‖ reconstructs D2 everywhere (0.977–0.983) and D1 in F2, F3 and F6 (0.90–0.95) but **not in F1 and F4** (0.41 and 0.35), where flexible models on all telemetry still give 0.97 and 0.96. D1_rel and D3, reported but outside the fixed reading, stay below 0.90 in some families even with GBDT on all telemetry (D1_rel 0.74 in F3 and 0.82 in F6; D3 0.80 in F6). They lie outside the fixed reading because G2, as defined in protocol v0.4, is computed by cross-fitting on calibration runs of the same schedule families (F2–F3), not with a family left out; values obtained with a family left out describe transfer and cannot open H1. (3) By interval length, the one-scalar reconstruction of D1 is weakest for the shortest intervals (R² 0.78 at 64–127 steps). (4) On the LM arm, a linear model on Ση + Ση‖m̂‖ trained on one configuration transfers to the other three at 0.956–0.971. Source: `runs/td_audit.log`. Reading under the fixed rule: robust, with a limited exception (D1 with a single scalar in F1/F4).
95: 
96: **T-B, the functional clock D3 under dosed label noise** (preregistered; 40 new runs, seeds 9101–9108). D3 and D1_rel were the candidates with an apparent exception (F6 left out of training, one seed: GBDT R² 0.81 and 0.79; `runs/tech4_analysis.log`); D3 was the one preregistered for T-B. On 8 new seeds per condition, leaving one seed out within the condition, GBDT on telemetry plus the same-cost competitors reconstructs D3 at R² 0.991 at noise 0.3 (minimum 0.968; 0 of 8 seeds below 0.90; ΔN + C10c alone 0.958), and at ≥ 0.990 at noise 0, 0.1, 0.5 and in F1. D1_rel, reported from the same runs, is reconstructed by the same GBDT at 0.993–0.994 in every condition. The dose–response criterion passes formally (unexplained share 0.003 / 0.004 / 0.009 / 0.010, monotone) but the unexplained variance stays ≤ 1%. **G2 fails for D3; by the preregistered rule H1 was not run.** Source: `runs/tb_part1_g2.log`, `runs/tb_part1_verdict.json`. The earlier exception thus arises only when F6 is absent from training; once F6 is sampled, D3 is reconstructed. Transfer to a schedule family left out of training remains limited (T-D).
97: 
98: **T-C, D3 on the language model** (criteria fixed before computing). The same-cost competitor C10c_LM (the change of loss on each probe's never-trained random answer word) reconstructs D3 at mean R² 0.979–0.988 alone and 0.980–0.989 with two further competitors, in all four LM configurations (minimum over seeds 0.979). Verdict: reconstructed. Source: `runs/tc_lm_d3_competitors.log`.
99: 
100: **The τ clock on the LM arm (port).** Held-out R² (leave one seed out) of the rate of τ in 8-step windows: Ση alone 0.58–0.69 (a pre-fixed prediction that the schedule would explain ≥ 0.80 failed); Ση‖m̂‖ 0.94–0.96; Ση + Ση‖m̂‖ 0.96–0.97; GBDT on all telemetry 0.976–0.988, in four configurations (fp16/bf16, α 16/32). Source: `runs/lmtech1_g2.log`, `lmtech2_g2.log`, `lmsens_bf16_g2.log`, `lmsens_a32_g2.log` (colloquium dossier of 7 Nov, R5, R6, R9).
101: 
102: **SuperNI, S1** (preregistered; 3 runs, 128 windows). OLS on Ση + Ση‖m̂‖ reconstructs the rate of τ at R² 0.986 with one seed left out (minimum 0.985) and **0.980 with a whole task left out** (minimum 0.969 over five heterogeneous tasks). Ση‖m̂‖ alone: 0.963 / 0.956; Ση alone: 0.51 / 0.44; GBDT: 0.94 / 0.72 (it extrapolates poorly to unseen tasks). Verdict: the reconstruction extends to natural tasks. Source: `runs/sni_S1_analysis.log`, `runs/sni_verdict.json`.
103: 
104: **Authors' code** (secondary, preregistered script). On R2's run, Σ‖Δθ‖ from the increments of Ση‖m̂‖ in 8-step windows: pooled R² 0.922 (351 windows), but by task 0.937 (dbpedia, no replay), 0.986, 0.934 and **0.578 (agnews)**. Post hoc, on the tasks with replay only: **0.749** (117 windows). Source: `runs/forever_repro_r2_reconstruction.log`, `runs/forever_repetari_r2_eta_m.json`. Analysis (d), fixed before running and without new training: on the same trajectory, the ratio Σ‖Δθ‖ / Ση‖m̂‖ drifts within a task (the ratio in the calibration window divided by the ratio over the rest of the task: 0.967, 0.861, 0.705 on amazon, yahoo, agnews; post hoc), so the 24-step calibration freezes a factor that later changes. This is a hypothesis about the mechanism, not a finding. In the repeated Ση‖m̂‖ arm of stage 1 (`eta_m_A1`, task-0 adapter of R1), the same computation on tasks 1–3, preregistered for this arm, gives **0.969** (amazon 0.986, yahoo 0.975, agnews 0.963; `runs/forever_repetari_r2_eta_m_A1.json`): the weak value of R2 did not recur. The calibration-to-rest ratio of that arm, computed post hoc in the same way, drifts in the same direction but less (0.966, 0.903, 0.810; both arms: `Stadiul0/forever_repro/drift_ratio_posthoc.py`, `runs/forever_drift_ratio_posthoc.log`; the τ runs do not log the Ση‖m̂‖ clock, so the ratio cannot be computed there). The drift hypothesis therefore remains compatible with two runs, while the weak R² on agnews belongs to one run.
105: 
106: ### 5.2 Scheduling: does the τ clock schedule replay better than a free clock?
107: 
108: **T-A, port, synthetic facts** (preregistered; 4 clocks × seeds 7301–7305, bf16; margin ±2 points on final mean accuracy).
109: 
110: | τ versus | mean difference in final accuracy | paired 95% CI | verdict | triggers |
111: |---|---|---|---|---|
112: | Ση‖m̂‖ | −0.0037 | [−0.0147; +0.0074] | practical equivalence | practically identical (median \|Δ\| 0 / 0 / 1 step) |
113: | Ση (schedule only) | +0.0023 | [−0.0041; +0.0086] | practical equivalence | differ (9 steps at d = 4; 36 vs 27 replays) |
114: | steps | −0.0018 | [−0.0106; +0.0070] | practical equivalence | — |
115: 
116: Mean final accuracy 0.240–0.246 in all arms; BWT ≈ −0.83. Source: `runs/ta_analysis.log`, `runs/ta_verdict.json`. Retention is low in this regime (memory of 16 facts out of 512 per task), so equivalence is shown under strong forgetting only. With 16 of 512 facts per task in memory, most of each task cannot be replayed at all, which limits how much the timing of replay can matter. Between τ and Ση‖m̂‖ the triggers practically coincided, so their equivalence says little; the informative contrasts are the schedule-only clock and the step count, which triggered differently.
117: 
118: **Post hoc (b): retention of the facts held in memory** (after the final review round; not preregistered). T-A stored only per-task accuracies, so the stored final LoRA factors of the 20 runs were loaded again on the CPU and the 16 memory facts of each of tasks 0–8 re-scored (`Stadiul0/lm/ta_memory_facts_posthoc.py`, `Stadiul0/ta_memory_posthoc.py`, `runs/ta_memory_posthoc.log`). On one complete run, the CPU recomputation differs from the stored GPU counts by 0–7 of 512 facts per task. Every clock recalls the memory facts almost perfectly (τ 0.9986, one of 720 facts missed; Ση‖m̂‖, Ση and steps 1.0000), against 0.134–0.138 for the other facts of the same tasks; τ minus each other clock −0.0014, paired 95% CI [−0.0052; +0.0025]. Replayed facts are thus kept whatever the clock, and the others are mostly lost, so in this regime the timing of replay could change little.
119: 
120: **SuperNI, S2** (preregistered; 3 seeds; margin ±5% on test loss). Relative difference τ − Ση‖m̂‖: −0.6%, 95% CI [−1.9%; +0.7%]: practical equivalence. Replay triggers coincided (median difference in trigger step 0 in each seed; 7 replays in both arms). S2 therefore does not test a difference in timing. Source: `runs/sni_analysis_final.log`, `runs/sni_verdict.json`, `runs/sni_S2_secundar.log` (recomputed byte for byte by `Stadiul0/sni_s2_secondary.py`).
121: 
122: **Authors' code, R1: reproduction** (criterion: overall performance OP within ±5 points of the paper's 72.9). OP 72.37 (dbpedia 93.2, amazon 47.5, yahoo 60.3, agnews 88.4); BWT −5.78 (paper −4.7). **FOREVER reproduced** (difference −0.53). The paper's figure is a mean over three orders; this is one run on one order. Source: `runs/forever_repro_eval_tau.log`.
123: 
124: **Authors' code, R2: τ replaced by Ση‖m̂‖** (criterion: |ΔOP| ≤ 2, descriptive; not a statistical equivalence test).
125: 
126: | | τ (R1) | Ση‖m̂‖ (R2) | τ − Ση‖m̂‖ |
127: |---|---|---|---|
128: | OP (exact match) | 72.37 | 71.53 | +0.84 |
129: | dbpedia | 93.21 | 87.89 | +5.33 |
130: | amazon | 47.52 | 48.92 | −1.40 |
131: | yahoo | 60.34 | 61.66 | −1.32 |
132: | agnews | 88.40 | 87.65 | +0.75 |
133: | BWT | −5.78 | −6.47 | +0.69 |
134: 
135: Source: `runs/forever_repro_eval_tau.log`, `runs/forever_repro_eval_eta_m.log`. Both aggregate differences are inside the preregistered margin. Ση‖m̂‖ triggered the same number of replays, at the same step or later (up to 33 of 313 steps; the 36 of stage 1 compares runs from the same task-0 adapter). The aggregate hides a 5.33-point difference on dbpedia in τ's favour (forgetting −4.8 under τ against −9.9 under Ση‖m̂‖), partly offset on amazon and yahoo. With one pair of runs, the clock cannot be separated from run-to-run non-determinism of fp16 training (the evaluation itself proved deterministic; post hoc (a) below).
136: 
137: **Repeated runs, stage 1** (preregistered v3; 2 × 2 crossed design, clock × task-0 adapter, the adapters of R1 and R2 reused so that the pairs share a starting point; margin 2 points on dbpedia). A = task-0 adapter of R1, B = that of R2; the two differ only by GPU non-determinism in task 0 (dbpedia right after it: 98.04 and 97.86). Each new arm copies the task-0 folder and runs tasks 1–3 with the authors' commands.
138: 
139: | Arm | Clock | Task 0 | dbpedia | amazon | yahoo | agnews | OP | BWT |
140: |---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
141: | R1 (`tau`) | τ | A | 93.21 | 47.52 | 60.34 | 88.40 | 72.37 | −5.78 |
142: | `eta_m_A1` | Ση‖m̂‖ | A | 93.73 | 49.36 | 61.86 | 87.20 | 73.04 | −4.79 |
143: | `tau_B1` | τ | B | 91.17 | 49.84 | 61.76 | 87.45 | 72.56 | −4.69 |
144: | R2 (`eta_m`) | Ση‖m̂‖ | B | 87.89 | 48.92 | 61.66 | 87.65 | 71.53 | −6.47 |
145: 
146: Differences are computed from unrounded values. Pairs sharing a task-0 adapter, τ − Ση‖m̂‖: on dbpedia (the quantity of the verdict) D_A1 = −0.51 and D_B1 = +3.29; OP −0.67 and +1.03; BWT −0.99 and +1.78. **Verdict under the rule fixed beforehand: not reproduced robustly.** The signs are opposite, so the data do not support a stable attribution of the dbpedia difference to the clock. This does not mean that the clock has no effect: with one run per cell, run-to-run noise of training and an interaction between clock and starting point cannot be separated. Stage 2 is not needed for the verdict and was not run. In all three pairs (R1–R2, A1, B1) the differences in OP and BWT stay within ±2 points. Descriptively, changing the task-0 adapter from A to B moved final dbpedia by 2.04 points under τ and by 5.84 under Ση‖m̂‖. Across the four cells, final dbpedia spans 5.84 points and OP 1.51.
147: 
148: **Post hoc (a): evaluation noise** (after the final review round; not preregistered). The final adapters of the four cells were evaluated again with the authors' unchanged generation and scoring scripts, twice with every random generator seeded (seeds 1 and 2) and once with greedy decoding: 12 evaluations of 16,500 test items each (`Stadiul0/forever_repro/eval_noise_posthoc.py`, `launch_eval_noise_posthoc.sh`, `eval_noise_summary.py`; `runs/forever_eval_noise_posthoc.json`). Their decoding sets `do_sample=True` but also temperature 0.02, top_k 1 and top_p 0, which keep only the most probable token. Both seeded evaluations reproduced every answer of the original evaluation in all four cells, so the standard deviation of the evaluation is 0 on every task. Greedy decoding changed 0–11 answers per task (46 in all) and the accuracy by at most 0.12 points: in each of the 46 items, at the first token where the two decodings part, the two highest fp16 logits are exactly equal, and the two procedures break the tie differently (`Stadiul0/forever_repro/eval_tie_diagnostic_posthoc.py`, `runs/forever_eval_tie_diagnostic_posthoc.json`; GPU, 10 Nov), and gave D_A1 = −0.47 and D_B1 = +3.33. The differences between cells therefore come from training (fp16 non-determinism, the clock, or both), not from the evaluation.
149: 
150: Replay triggers (step at which the clock crossed each threshold; the first is at step 24 by construction):
151: 
152: | Task | τ, R1 (A) | Ση‖m̂‖, A1 | τ, B1 | Ση‖m̂‖, R2 (B) |
153: |---|---|---|---|---|
154: | amazon | 24, 48, 100, 185 | 24, 50, 105, 196 | 24, 48, 100, 183 | 24, 50, 104, 192 |
155: | yahoo | 24, 46, 87, 160 | 24, 47, 90, 165 | 24, 46, 90, 164 | 24, 48, 96, 178 |
156: | agnews | 24, 47, 93, 160 | 24, 50, 105, — | 24, 47, 90, 157 | 24, 47, 96, 193 |
157: 
158: Within each pair, Ση‖m̂‖ triggered later than τ in 16 of the 17 comparable triggers, once at the same step and never earlier (0–36 steps), counting the thresholds after the first (at step 24 by construction) that both clocks reached: 8 in pair A and 9 in pair B. Each of tasks 1–3 has 313 steps. The two τ runs, which start from different adapters, differ by at most 4 steps. On agnews with adapter A, Ση‖m̂‖ did not reach the fourth threshold before the end of the task, so it made one clock-triggered replay fewer. The delay is systematic, as analysis (d) found on a single trajectory; in these two pairs it did not translate into a consistent difference in retention. Integrity checks: the copied task-0 adapters carry the hashes of their sources, the files of R1 and R2 were not modified, every stage returned 0. Source: `runs/forever_repetari_verdict.json`, `runs/forever_repro_eval_*.log`, `Stadiul0/forever_repro/logs/*_train_t*.log`.
159: 
160: ### 5.3 What each clock costs
161: 
162: The four clocks compared as replay triggers differ in what they compute at every training step. Listed from the code (`Stadiul0/lm/lm_forever_run.py`; the clock-swap variant of FOREVER's script); the run time of each was not measured separately.
163: 
164: | Clock | Computed at each step | Extra memory | Reads the model's parameters? |
165: |---|---|---|---|
166: | Steps | a counter | none | no |
167: | Ση (applied learning rate) | the scheduler's current rate, added to a sum | none | no |
168: | Ση‖m̂‖ | the norm of Adam's first moment, already kept by the optimizer, with its bias correction, times the rate | none beyond the optimizer's own state | no |
169: | τ = Σ‖Δθ‖ (FOREVER) | the difference between the trainable parameters and their copy from the previous step, and its norm | one copy of all trainable parameters | yes |
170: 
171: For the LoRA set-up of FOREVER on Qwen3-0.6B (r 8 on q_proj and v_proj), the trainable parameters number 1,146,880, so τ's copy takes about 4.6 MB in fp32, which is small. Under full fine-tuning, which FOREVER also reports, the copy would be the size of the whole set of trained weights. The practical advantage of Ση‖m̂‖ is therefore modest but definite: it uses only what the optimizer already holds. This holds for the clock. In our variants FOREVER's replay strength β still uses ‖Δθ‖, so removing the copy altogether would also require replacing β, for instance by η‖m̂‖, which was not tested.
172: 
173: The copy is not needed even to compute τ exactly. In AdamW (PyTorch 2.8, which FOREVER's Trainer uses, with weight decay 0 as in both implementations here) each coordinate moves by Δθ_i = −η (m̂_i/(√v̂_i + ε) + λθ_i), where m̂ and v̂ are the bias-corrected moments the optimizer already holds and λ is the decoupled weight decay. So ‖Δθ‖ can be computed from the optimizer's state at the step itself: τ is not only reconstructed from that state, it is determined by it, up to floating-point rounding (a step skipped by the GradScaler changes neither the parameters nor the state). The same holds for any optimizer that computes its step from its own state: ‖Δθ‖ = η‖g‖ for plain SGD, η‖velocity‖ with momentum, and for optimizers whose step size is normalised by construction τ is close to the summed learning rate. The same computation would give FOREVER's replay strength β, which uses the same ‖Δθ‖, without the copy; neither computation was implemented here. What the tests of §5.1 and §5.2 measured is therefore different: whether one cheap scalar that is logged anyway suffices, and whether a free clock can replace τ as a replay scheduler. These identities follow from the update rules; they were not tested here.
174: 
175: ## 6. What this settles, and what it does not
176: 
177: **Not identifiable, in this framework.** In our implementations, every candidate for c_e tested here (D1, D1_rel, D2, D3 and FOREVER's τ) is reconstructed out of sample from cheap telemetry or same-cost competitors (R² ≥ 0.90; the exceptions named in §5.1 all come from extrapolation to a schedule family left out of training, and those of D3 and D1_rel in F6 did not recur in T-B, where F6 itself was sampled with new seeds and one seed was left out; reconstruction thus has to be learned within the regime, and transfer to a family left out of training remains limited), so the hypothesis that c_e predicts forgetting beyond telemetry is not identifiable here. Used as a replay scheduler, τ gave no measurable advantage over free clocks in our port, on synthetic facts and on SuperNI. With the authors' code, in three pairs of runs, OP and BWT stayed within ±2 points, and the 5.3-point difference on the first task did not recur consistently from common starting points (§5.2). Perspective's Level 2 is revised accordingly (Ionici 2026d): c_e remains a definition, and its cheap candidates are not identifiable in the regimes tested. G2 is an operational threshold fixed in advance, not a proof: whether the remainder (up to 10% of the variance of the rate; at most 1% for D3 in T-B) predicts forgetting was not tested, because the forgetting targets were never opened.
178: 
179: **A reading of the scheduling results.** In the regimes tested, the exact moment of replay, within the range produced by the clocks compared, did not change retention measurably. In T-A the schedule-only clock and the step count triggered replay up to 9 and 13 steps away from τ (median differences), and the schedule-only clock made 36 replays per run on average against 27 for τ (`runs/ta_analysis.log`), yet every paired 95% interval of the difference in final accuracy stayed within ±0.015; post hoc, the replayed facts were recalled almost perfectly under every clock (§5.2). With the authors' code, Ση‖m̂‖ triggered later than τ in both pairs of stage 1 (up to 36 steps) and, on one task, made one replay fewer, yet the dbpedia difference had opposite signs in the two pairs (−0.51 and +3.29 points) and OP stayed within ±2. This is an observation about these regimes, not evidence for a class of clocks defined by general conditions: no such conditions were fixed in advance; the step count, which tracks no change in the model at all, did as well as τ in T-A; T-A had strong forgetting (final accuracy about 0.24) and a small memory; and on the authors' benchmark the paper reports +1.2 points of overall performance (+1.1 BWT) for τ over a step-based calibration (Table 2, one benchmark), a comparison not repeated here with their code.
180: 
181: **Not settled.** (i) Whether the systematic delay of Ση‖m̂‖ in the authors' fp16 code has an effect on retention smaller than the run-to-run variation of training seen here (several points on one task; the evaluation is deterministic, post hoc (a)): settling that would need many runs per cell, other seeds and other orders. (ii) Larger models, full fine-tuning, other optimizers, longer task sequences. (iii) Forgetting regimes with good retention for the synthetic-fact comparison (T-A had strong forgetting). (iv) Candidates outside this list (for example representation-similarity measures, listed as secondary and never tested).
182: 
183: **What would overturn or bound these conclusions.** Each item is a test that someone else can run with the archived code and rules. Inside this framework, a positive outcome would overturn the corresponding verdict; in another setting it would mark a limit of it.
184: 1. *Identifiability.* A setting in which a candidate's rate is not reconstructed out of sample, with R² < 0.90 for both the linear and the flexible model on the same telemetry and same-cost competitors (gate G2), and without extrapolation to a schedule family left out of training. G2 would then pass, and H1 could be tested.
185: 2. *Scheduling.* A preregistered comparison with a margin of 2 points in which τ beats Ση‖m̂‖, or the schedule-only clock, with a mean difference of at least 2 points and a paired 95% interval above zero (the criterion of T-A).
186: 3. *The authors' code.* A difference on one task that keeps its sign and size across many runs per cell, over other seeds and task orders. This is the test that stage 1 could not settle with one run per cell.
187: 
188: **On generality.** Two predictions follow; neither was tested here.
189: - *Same configuration, other hardware.* The numbers will differ; the verdicts should not. Most of them lie well clear of their thresholds; for τ on the LM arm and on SuperNI, the reconstruction R² is 0.94–0.99, against 0.90. Two lie closest. D3 in the technical rounds had R² 0.91–0.96 (§5.1, first table) and was re-tested in T-B at 0.99. Among the scheduling verdicts, T-A has a widest interval bound of −0.0147, against a margin of ±0.02.
190: - *Larger models with Adam-type optimizers.* τ remains determined by the optimizer's own state at any scale, since each step is computed from it (§2, §5.3). Whether one cheap scalar still suffices is open: under full fine-tuning, τ runs over all weights and the second moments are more heterogeneous.
191: 
192: The equivalence of the clocks as replay schedulers has no such mechanistic basis and should not be extrapolated beyond the regimes tested.
193: 
194: ## 7. Limitations
195: 
196: - **Local preregistration.** Hashes were recorded before each run, but not deposited publicly; an outside reader must trust the records. Every preregistration, hash list and log is in the archive with its timestamps. Some files named in the hash lists were changed afterwards for a later test; the earlier versions are archived (11 of 13; four of them reconstructed and identified by their recorded hash; the two not recovered are the LM training script of lmtech1 and lmtech2 (below) and a first version of td_audit.py, replaced one minute later, whose output is archived), and `Stadiul0/verify_hash_lists.py` checks every entry.
197: - **Two code versions not preserved.** The synthetic-world code of the technical rounds of 4 Nov (before the corrections of 6 Nov) and the LM training script of the runs lmtech1 and lmtech2 were not kept. Their stored results, resolved configurations and (for the LM runs) the script's hash are archived, and the analyses reproduce from them; the runs themselves cannot be repeated with exactly that code.
198: - **Small samples.** T-A 5 seeds per clock; SuperNI 3 seeds; the authors' code one pair (R1, R2) plus two crossed runs from shared task-0 adapters (stage 1), one run per cell; seed 42 throughout, as fixed in their code; one task order (order 1 of the paper).
199: - **Scope.** Models of 0.93 M and 0.6 B parameters; LoRA on q_proj and v_proj only; synthetic facts, five SuperNI tasks, four classification tasks.
200: - **Several analyses are post hoc** and labelled as such (the R² 0.749 on replay tasks, the drift ratios of (d) and of stage 1, residual analyses, and checks (a)–(c) made after the final review round).
201: - **Many comparisons** without correction; verdicts follow the fixed rules, and secondary numbers are descriptive.
202: - **Equivalence margins are conventions** (±2 points; ±5% on loss), not derived from a smallest effect of interest, and no power analysis was made. Only T-A and S2 use paired 95% confidence intervals, with few seeds; inclusion of such an interval in the margin amounts to two one-sided tests at α = 0.025 each. Their widest bounds (−0.0147 on accuracy; −1.9% on loss) indicate the size of effect the data can exclude.
203: - **Evaluation noise** in the authors' code was measured post hoc and is zero: their decoding keeps only the most probable token, and two seeded re-evaluations of the four final adapters reproduced every answer (§5.2). What remains is the non-determinism of fp16 training, which one run per cell cannot measure.
204: - **Hardware.** One laptop GPU with 4 GB, against the 8 NVIDIA H20 data-centre GPUs of the authors (Appendix H); the allocator change for tasks 1–3 of the authors' code; GPU non-determinism in fp16. Runs of their fp16 code are not expected to be bit-identical across such different hardware, so the reproduction (OP within 0.53 points) and the run-to-run differences are read against margins, not as exact replications. In all, the runs kept the laptop GPU busy for 63.5 hours in three sessions (6–10 Nov), and the hardware watchdog never stopped one (times per test: archive `README.md`).
205: - **Our code on one machine only.** The synthetic world and the LM arm ran only on the author's laptop, where they are bit-identical on repetition. On other hardware, agreement is expected at the level of the verdicts, not of the numbers (§6), but this was not tested.
206: - **Direction decisions** were taken through colloquia of AI systems under rules fixed before reading their answers, with every claim verified; the author is not a machine-learning specialist (§9).
207: 
208: ## 8. Notes on FOREVER (for its authors and users)
209: 
210: Neutral observations made while reproducing the method; none changes the reproduced score.
211: 1. Every phase (calibration, current-task training, replay) builds a new `transformers.Trainer`, so AdamW, the learning-rate schedule with warmup and the fp16 GradScaler restart at each phase, and the data loader starts again from the beginning; τ therefore contains the schedule directly. In the drift logs the first step of every phase (step 1, step 25 and the step after each trigger) has Δθ = 0, as do a few other steps (consistent with steps skipped by the GradScaler, which the code does not log; at the first step of a phase the new optimizer holds no moments yet). The free clock adds nothing at those first steps, and at the three other such steps of the two η‖m̂‖ runs it adds at most 0.3% of the clock after calibration and 1.9% of one calibration window (post hoc (c): `Stadiul0/forever_repro/skip_check_posthoc.py`, `runs/forever_skip_check_posthoc.log`; first written for the final review round), so skipped steps do not explain note 7.
212: 2. The code uses LoRA α = 16 and batch 32 (4 × 8); the paper states α = 32 and batch 8 (Appendix H).
213: 3. The launch script calls `finetune_cl_ours_qwen.py`, which is not in the repository; `finetune_cl_our_qwen.py` is.
214: 4. The generators hard-code the SuperNI test folder, so the "long sequence" benchmark cannot be evaluated without a change.
215: 5. Memory sampling takes at least one example per distinct answer; on generation tasks with mostly distinct answers this approaches the whole task rather than 2% (observation from the code; not tested at scale).
216: 6. Overall performance is computed by exact match and BWT with tolerant label extraction; decoding sets `do_sample=True` with temperature 0.02, top_k 1 and top_p 0, which keeps only the most probable token, so it is deterministic in practice and needs no seed (post hoc (a), §5.2).
217: 7. In our re-implementation (bf16 without GradScaler, one data stream across a task's phases) the free clock Ση‖m̂‖ triggered replay at practically the same steps as τ (median difference ≤ 1 step); in the original code it triggered at the same step or later, in all three pairs of runs (up to 36 of 313 steps; in two pairs from shared starting points, 16 of 17 comparable triggers later and none earlier), and on the same trajectory the ratio between the two clocks drifts within a task. Which difference causes this was not established. Our re-implementation also restarts AdamW, the schedule and the GradScaler at every phase, so these restarts alone do not explain it. The remaining differences changed together: the data loader restarting at every phase against one stream; fp16 with GradScaler against bf16; accumulation of 4 × 8 against one batch of 32; the loss over the sequence against the loss on the answer token; and the Trainer's own settings.
218: 8. The paper's ablation of the time signal is against step counts only; the learning-rate schedule and Ση‖m̂‖ are free competitors that the reproduction suggests including.
219: 9. At the scale of the paper, the decisive check of the clock costs almost nothing. Log Ση‖m̂‖ next to τ: it is the learning rate times the norm of Adam's bias-corrected first moment, which the optimizer already holds. Then compute the R² of the rate of τ from it in 8-step windows (`Stadiul0/forever_repro/r2_reconstruction.py` does this for one run). The same quantity can also enter the ablation as a replay clock: `Stadiul0/forever_repro/make_clock_variant.py` builds that variant from their script and also logs τ. The fp16 code is not deterministic, so comparing clocks needs several runs per configuration.
220: 
221: ## 9. How the work was done
222: 
223: The research question and the framework it tests are the author's (Ionici 2026c). The author also set how the study was governed: decision rules fixed in advance, colloquia with claim-by-claim verification, and consent for each action. The author provided and maintained the hardware, and paid for the external evaluations that were not free. The author approved the tests proposed by the colloquia and each decision rule, which was written and hashed before any answer was read, and kept the right to stop or postpone at every step. Runs were approved one campaign at a time, except for the technical rounds of 6–8 November, which ran under a standing authorisation limited to that weekend and to a hardware watchdog. Direction calls were settled by the rules applied to the verified answers, not by the author's preference.
224: 
225: An AI assistant, Claude Opus 5.5 (Anthropic; model identifier claude-opus-5-5), used through Claude Code in the Claude desktop application in October and November 2026, worked under the author's direction. It wrote and ran the code that produced all the data, ran the analyses, monitored the hardware, and checked every number and reference against the logs and the sources. It also built the archive and its reproduction checks, and drafted this text. The author reviewed the results, the decision documents and the text; the code was not reviewed line by line by the author, and its correctness rests on the automated checks, the preregistered analyses and the reproduction from the archive (§10). The author is responsible for all of it.
226: 
227: Direction calls were put to colloquia of AI systems (6 November: five participants and one non-voting witness; 8 November: three participants and one witness, named in `Colocviu/README.md`). Each system received the same dossier, sent by the assistant through the author's own accounts after the author approved each sending. Every factual claim and reference in the answers was checked, and recommendations resting on a false claim were struck out. The colloquia contributed substantively. The four decisive tests were proposed in their answers: T-A by all five participants of 6 November, T-B by four, and T-C and T-D by three each. Three errors or omissions of the dossier were found through them (`Colocviu/Verificare_raspunsuri_2026-11-06.md`). Dossiers, rules, answers and verification documents are in the archive (`Colocviu/`). The earlier AI evaluations of the protocol drafts (v0.1–v0.4), the prompts sent and their verification are kept in `Recenzii/` as an informative annex. They document the path of the study (its dilemmas, obstacles and how they were resolved) but support none of its conclusions. No AI system is listed as an author, because none can take responsibility for the work.
228: 
229: ## 10. Data and code availability
230: 
231: Code, preregistrations, hash lists, logs and results: [DOI-ARCHIVE] and https://github.com/danionici-creator/pilot2-model-clock-audit (code under MIT, data and documents under CC BY 4.0). Large run arrays and the LoRA adapters: [DOI-ARCHIVE], data part. Not redistributed: FOREVER's code and data (no licence; `Stadiul0/forever_repro/prepare_forever_copy.py` rebuilds the exact working copy from the authors' commit and checks it against the hashes recorded before the runs), model weights (Hugging Face; hashes in `Stadiul0/modele_provenienta.md`), hardware reports. The archive opens with `INDEX.md`, which sorts every file into essential and non-essential for repeating the test and maps each test to its rules, launcher, code, stored results and analysis. The 22 analyses behind the verdicts, tables and quoted numbers of this report (19, plus the post hoc checks (a)–(c)) run again from the archive with `Stadiul0/reproduce_analyses.py` (CPU, about 3 minutes). On 10 Nov 2026, from a fresh copy of the archive with the data part unpacked, all 22 reproduced the stored outputs: every log line for line, and the JSON files identical or with relative differences below 1e-15.

22: work omitted from version 1.0 [33, 53, 54, 62]. The hypothesis is unchanged in every version;

25: not identifiable in the regimes tested
26: DOI [DOI-PERSPECTIVE-1.4]
27: Relation Accompanies two completed studies, whose empirical claims it does not extend: an exploratory 
28: study [25], whose design principles for evaluation it uses, and a study of Level 2 [24], whose 
29: finding that the cheapest candidates are not identifiable it reports (§4, §5)
30: Scope Functional properties of persistent artificial agents. No claim is made about consciousness, moral 
31: status, or personal identity
32: License © 2026 Ioan Daniel Ionici. Creative Commons Attribution 4.0 International (CC BY 4.0)
33: Abstract
34: Persistent artificial agents are anchored in time from outside, and whatever timing they learn is  
35: part of a state that can be saved and restored: from that state alone, an agent cannot tell whether  
36: it has continued or been rolled back. We classify the temporal evidence available to an agent by  
37: who can write, reset, forge, or copy it and whether its self-model can read it, and we distinguish  
38: continuity claims adjudicated by the platform from raw evidence interpreted by the agent. Our  
39: hypothesis is conditional: knowing how old one’s knowledge is, detecting suspension, restoration, 
40: or copying, and ordering one’s own changes benefit from authenticated evidence that is not  
41: restored with the agent’s state, chiefly when external records are absent, delayed, or untrusted;  
42: with complete, trusted logs, replay reconstructs any deterministic agent. Physical irreversibility  
43: is one route to such evidence, not the argument for it. We separate two roles: a non-restorable  
44: time reference with a state-bound operation counter distinguishes suspension from restoration,  
45: whereas an index of accumulated experience-driven change, read against that reference, serves  
46: 
47: PAGE 2
48: A time of their own: agent-held temporal evidence
49: I. D. Ionici · Perspective, version 1.4 · [DATA PUBLICĂRII] November 2026 2
50: the agent’s model of its own adaptation. Each test is paired with a conventional competitor that  
51: can  close  it,  and  access  to  evidence  of  restoration  confers  no  control  over  it.  A  completed 
52: exploratory study supplies evaluation design principles but does not test the programme. A  
53: second study tested the cheapest candidates for the index, including a published update-norm  
54: clock for replay in language models: in small models trained with Adam, each was reconstructed  
55: from free optimizer telemetry or from a competitor of the same cost, and as a replay clock the  
56: update-norm index did no better than a free one in the regimes tested. The index remains a  
57: definition, its cheap candidates are not identifiable, and the continuity anchor is untested. We  
58: make no claim about consciousness or moral status.
59: Keywords: agent-held temporal evidence; persistent agents; self-models; state continuity; rollback; irreversibility; time 
60: perception; provenance; hardware roots of trust.
61: What this perspective offers
62: • A classification of the temporal evidence available to an agent — of order, duration, sequential computation, 
63: continuity, and its own change — by who can write, reset, forge, or copy it, and by whether the agent’s self-
64: model can read it, under explicit threat models (Table 1).
65: • An elementary result, given a machine-checked form in earlier work [54], that locates the problem: an agent 
66: whose complete restorable state is restored cannot detect the restoration from that state alone; detection  
67: requires authenticated evidence that was not restored with it and to which the restored state is bound (§3).
68: • A separation of two roles: a continuity anchor — a non-restorable time reference and a state-bound  
69: operation counter — detects and distinguishes suspension and restoration, whereas a dual clock relating  
70: accumulated experience-driven change to that reference serves the agent’s model of its own adaptation  
71: (§4).
72: • A sequence of tests, each with the conventional mechanism that could make it unnecessary (Table 2), self-
73: contained definitions of its methodological gates, and a design principle that separates access to evidence of 
74: restoration from control over it.
75: 1. Introduction
76: Persistent artificial agents are ordinarily anchored in time from outside. Their timestamps, logs,  
77: checkpoints, and version identifiers record when things happened, and a language model knows  
78: the current date only if the date is written into its input; even the age of its own knowledge is  
79: uncertain, because the effective cutoff of what it learned can differ from the reported one [12].  
80: Agents can also learn timing: reinforcement-learning agents develop internal time-keeping when 
81: timing  matters  for  the  task  [48,  49].  But  whatever  timing  an  agent  learns  is  part  of  its 
82: computational state, and that state can be saved, copied, and restored. This becomes a limitation  
83: once an agent persists, changes, and must reason about its own past: how long ago it learned  
84: something, whether it has been paused, restored, or copied, in what order its own changes  
85: occurred, and how an experience will change it.
86: The limitation is structural. In a system whose whole state can be saved and restored, every  
87: internal clock and every internal record is saved and restored with it. Such an agent holds no  
88: evidence about elapsed time or about its own change that it could not have been given, reset, or  
89: forged by whoever controls its state and inputs. Any count it keeps, any memory it consolidates,  
90: 
91: PAGE 3
92: A time of their own: agent-held temporal evidence
93: I. D. Ionici · Perspective, version 1.4 · [DATA PUBLICĂRII] November 2026 3
94: and any timestamp it reads can be rolled back together, and after a restoration nothing in the  
95: agent’s state shows that anything happened.
96: The concern is not new. Language-model agents have been described as “ontologically stateless”, 
97: with identifiability, continuity, and persistence undermined despite external memory and tools  
98: [50]; it has been argued that such an agent can say the right things about itself without the  
99: conditions that would make those statements meaningful co-occurring at the moment of decision, 
100: and persistence scores computable from scaffold traces have been proposed [51]; reverting a  
101: persistent  agent’s  self-description  after  its  memory  has  grown  does  not  restore  its  earlier 
102: behaviour [63]; identity for AI agents has been defined as a continuous relationship between what 
103: an agent is declared to be and what it is observed to do [43]; a security study of deployed agent  
104: systems shows that a faithfully restored checkpoint can still resume an invalid execution, because 
105: the state a checkpoint restores differs from the dependencies that secure continuation requires  
106: [70]; a survey of always-on agents finds the literature more concerned with accumulating and  
107: retrieving state than with governing, recovering, or relinquishing it [15]; and architectures have  
108: been  proposed  that  preserve  an  agent’s  identity,  memory,  and  code  lineage  across  models, 
109: harnesses, and hosts under explicit continuity invariants [73],  or that  verify  governed state  
110: transitions with a three-way verdict [21]. Temporal cognition has long been argued to be a key  
111: ingredient of intelligent systems [34]; robotic models of episodic and autobiographical memory  
112: have  been  proposed  as  a  synthetic temporal  self  [52];  and duration  can  be  estimated  from 
113: accumulated change in a network’s activity [57]. Similar intuitions — that AI systems lack a clock 
114: or a time of their own — have also been voiced informally; we do not rely on them as evidence.
115: What this perspective adds is a question about evidence rather than about identity: what can an  
116: agent know about elapsed time and about its own change, from which sources, and which of those 
117: sources could it not have been given, reset, or forged? The mechanisms that answer this question 
118: already exist, in hardware security, in cryptography, and in the neuroscience of timing. Three  
119: arrangements must be kept apart: ( a) continuity evidence held by the platform and used by  
120: operators, or by a protected module that checks the freshness of its state and halts on a mismatch 
121: [40, 47, 61] — the status quo; ( b) continuity claims adjudicated by a trusted monitor and passed  
122: to the agent as an authenticated result; and (c) raw evidence read by the agent’s self-model, which 
123: infers continuity, duration, and its own change itself. The proposal concerns (b) and (c), and (b) is 
124: also the strongest competitor of ( c). The individual mechanisms are established; the proposed  
125: contribution lies in their integration as authenticated temporal evidence available to an agent’s  
126: self-model, and in the falsifiable programme by which that integration can be evaluated.
127: Priority should be read narrowly. The mechanisms on which the proposal draws are established  
128: or adjacent: trusted counters, freshness checks, and rollback-resistant storage [36, 40, 47, 61];  
129: provenance-guided selective replay [71]; machine unlearning [8]; training-data attribution, which 
130: asks which example changed a model offline and from outside it [28, 46]; and the cumulative norm 
131: of parameter updates as a model clock [18]. The proposal is not a new clock, cryptographic  
132: primitive,  unlearning  method,  or  attribution  algorithm.  Measured  against  this  work,  the 
133: continuity anchor overlaps substantially with rollback protection, revocation and replay overlap  
134: substantially with unlearning and provenance-guided recovery, and source attribution by the  
135: agent overlaps partially with training-data attribution. What is plausibly new is the separation of  
136: arrangements (a), (b), and (c), the classification of the evidence in Table 1, and a programme in  
137: which each test is set against a conventional competitor that can close it.
138: 
139: PAGE 4
140: A time of their own: agent-held temporal evidence
141: I. D. Ionici · Perspective, version 1.4 · [DATA PUBLICĂRII] November 2026 4
142: The motivating intuition is simple to state: an artificial agent needs a connection to a time of its  
143: own, and that time must be irreversible. “A time of their own” is used here as shorthand for agent-
144: accessible evidence of elapsed time and continuity, not for subjective temporal experience or for  
145: proper time in the sense of relativity; the hypothesis of §2 is the testable form of the intuition. The 
146: fields cited throughout supply candidate mechanisms and adjacent formulations of the problem,  
147: not convergent evidence for the hypothesis.
148: This  perspective  accompanies  a  closed  exploratory  study  of  episode–trace  binding  [25].  It 
149: introduces no data and does not extend that study’s claims. What it takes from the study are the  
150: design principles of its evaluation, defined in §6 so that they can be used without the study, and  
151: two cautions that the study made concrete; the study itself tests none of the levels of §5. A second 
152: study, undertaken after version 1.2 and completed before this version, tested Level 2 for its  
153: cheapest candidates [24]. Its outcome is reported where that level is described (§4, Table 2), and  
154: it adds two cautions to §6.
155: 2. Agent-Held Temporal Evidence
156: By  temporal evidence  we mean information that bears on five distinct quantities: the  order of  
157: events; the duration that has elapsed in physical time; the minimum sequential computation that 
158: has taken place; the  continuity of the agent’s trajectory — whether it has been interrupted,  
159: restored, or forked; and the agent’s trajectory of change — how experience has altered it. Different 
160: mechanisms supply different quantities, and none supplies all of them.
161: A self-model is understood operationally: a component whose outputs are predictions about the  
162: agent itself — its future state, the change in its competences, the age of its information, the  
163: probability that it has been compromised, or the effect of an update or a rollback. Without such a  
164: measurable output, no component counts as a self-model here. Evidence is  agent-held if it is  
165: available to the self-model at decision time, and authenticated where it originates outside the  
166: agent’s state. It comes in two forms, corresponding to arrangements (b) and (c) of §1: adjudicated 
167: claims, in which a trusted monitor evaluates continuity and passes an authenticated result, and  
168: raw agent-bound evidence, which the self-model reads and interprets itself. Agent-held does not  
169: mean stored inside the checkpointable state; the point of the proposal is that some of it must not  
170: be.
171: Five architectural parts are therefore distinguished. The agent’s core state can be checkpointed,  
172: restored, and copied. Agent-bound evidence sources lie outside the checkpoint; the agent may read 
173: them but cannot write, reset, or decrement them, and the increments and state commitments they 
174: require are executed by a trusted monitor as part of each committed step. The host orchestrator 
175: controls storage, checkpoints, and restoration; the authority to restore legitimately belongs to the 
176: operator, exercised through policies that the trusted monitor enforces and records [9], so that  
177: under threat model B the host can execute a restoration but cannot make it authorised. The  
178: trusted reference monitor lies inside the trusted computing base, mediates the evidence sources,  
179: holds the keys with which their readings are authenticated, and executes the protocol-enforced  
180: increments. External channels carry messages that the agent must validate.
181: Claims about evidence depend on the threat model. We distinguish three: ( A) a benign operator  
182: and  accidental  faults  —  a  rollback  by  mistake,  a  wrong  timestamp,  a  stale  checkpoint,  an 
183: 
184: PAGE 5
185: A time of their own: agent-held temporal evidence
186: I. D. Ionici · Perspective, version 1.4 · [DATA PUBLICĂRII] November 2026 5
187: unrecorded suspension; (B) a privileged software adversary — in practice, a compromised host  
188: orchestrator — who controls storage, inputs, and checkpoints but cannot alter trusted hardware  
189: or  forge  its  attestation;  and  (C)  a  physical  adversary  who  controls  the  device  itself  —  its 
190: temperature and voltage, its replacement, and the channel through which a physical source is  
191: read. Under B, the agent’s path to the evidence must itself lie within the trusted computing base  
192: — the self-model executes in a trusted execution environment or reads through the monitor —  
193: because  a  host  adversary  could  otherwise  replay  old  readings  to  a  restored  agent  or  alter 
194: verification keys stored in the restorable state; freshness rests on that path and on the monitor’s  
195: own counter, not on nonces stored in the state, which roll back with it. The proposal is plausible  
196: under A and B. Under C, no local component guarantees anything by itself. Table 1 classifies the  
197: sources of temporal evidence under threat model B.
