346: Under threat model B the adversary controls the platform’s storage, inputs, and checkpoints but neither the physics of the device nor the  
347: trusted hardware, which moves the trust to its manufacturer. Under threat model C every row may be forged or replayed by whoever  
348: controls the device, except that a sequential-computation proof still bounds the work done since a fresh challenge.
349: Today a self-model reads the first row and, implicitly, the second. The last three rows hold  
350: evidence  that  an  adversary  of  type  B  cannot  have  reset  —  provided  that  the  challenges  of 
351: sequential proofs are fresh and that decay cells are re-armed only by trusted hardware. Protected 
352: modules already read trusted counters at execution time, but for deterministic guard checks that 
353: halt on a mismatch [47, 61]; the shift proposed here is to route such evidence, adjudicated or raw, 
354: into a learning agent’s predictive self-model.
355: Hypothesis. Some functions of a persistent agent benefit when evidence from the last three rows 
356: of  Table  1  is  agent-held,  chiefly  when  external  records  are  absent,  incomplete,  delayed,  or 
357: untrusted. Knowledge age and discontinuity detection rest on those rows directly; ordering and  
358: revocation, and anticipation, also require the agent’s records of its own trajectory and of the  
359: provenance of its changes, anchored in order and time by those rows:
360: • Knowledge age: estimating how much time has passed since something was learned, and  
361: hence how likely it is to be stale.
362: • Discontinuity  detection:  recognising  that  one’s  trajectory  has  been  interrupted  —  by 
363: suspension, by restoration to an earlier state, or by forking into copies — or that the  
364: timestamps one receives have been altered.
365: • Ordering and revocation : ordering one’s own changes without trusted timestamps, and  
366: locating and undoing the effect of an experience later found to be compromised [71]; an  
367: authorised revocation must be recorded as such, so that it is not mistaken for an adversarial 
368: rollback [9].
369: • Anticipation:  predicting  how,  and  how  durably,  an  experience  will  change  one’s  own 
370: competences.
371: What the hypothesis does not say.  It does not say that external records are informationally  
372: insufficient. With complete and trusted logs, replay reconstructs the state of any deterministic  
373: agent; an internal state is then a cache of the log, and its advantage, if any, is computational. The  
374: hypothesis concerns regimes — absent, incomplete, delayed, or untrusted records, and limits on  
375: compute and latency — not a capacity that logs could never supply. One possible outcome of the  
376: programme is therefore functional separation: continuity may be best guaranteed by the platform, 
377: with the agent modelling only adjudicated claims supplied to it. Raw agent-bound evidence would 
378: then be unnecessary, even if temporal self-modelling remained useful.
379: 3. Where Physics Enters
380: The  physical  premise  behind  the  motivating  intuition  is  true  but  proves  too  much.  Every 
381: implemented system follows a physical trajectory, and every record is a physical change: erasing  
382: information has an irreducible thermodynamic cost [31], and memory records in the direction in 
383: which entropy increases [38]. A log entry and a decaying capacitor are equally physical and  
384: equally  aligned  with  the  arrow  of  time.  That  a  physical  process  exists  therefore  does  not 
385: distinguish the proposal from the status quo.
386: 
387: PAGE 7
388: A time of their own: agent-held temporal evidence
389: I. D. Ionici · Perspective, version 1.4 · [DATA PUBLICĂRII] November 2026 7
390: What distinguishes it is restorability. Digital computation is engineered to be independent of its  
391: physical microstate: memory is refreshed against decay, errors are corrected, and runs can be  
392: reproduced bit for bit — as all one hundred training runs of the final iteration of the accompanying 
393: study were. The consequence can be stated as an elementary result.
394: Proposition  (rollback  indistinguishability). Let  an  agent’s  complete  restorable  state  — 
395: everything a checkpoint saves — be restored exactly, at time t1, to its value S(t0) at an earlier time 
396: t0. Any deterministic function of that state alone then takes the same value as it did at t0, and for 
397: an agent that draws fresh randomness from a memoryless source, the distribution of its outputs  
398: is  the  same  as  at  t0;  no  computation  over  the  restored  state  can  therefore  distinguish 
399: uninterrupted continuation from restoration. Restoration can be detected only by comparing the 
400: restored state with evidence that was not restored with it — a source outside the checkpoint or  
401: an authenticated message from outside — and only if the restored state carries an authenticated  
402: commitment to an earlier reading of that evidence, or the source holds one to the latest state, and 
403: the source and the channel through which it is read can be trusted.
404: The proposition is elementary, and it is not new. It underlies the state-continuity protocols of  
405: protected modules [47, 61]; a machine-checked statement of it for tamper-evident agent memory, 
406: under  the  same  name,  was  published  shortly  before  this  article  [54],  together  with  a 
407: generalisation — no mechanism can decide a property whose deciding fact is absent from its input 
408: [53]; and an analogous separation has been proved for the authority of an agent’s memory  
409: content, which defences based on content or derivation history cannot establish because both can 
410: be laundered, and which requires binding to the origin at write time [33]. What the proposition  
411: does here is locate the problem. The question is not whether an agent can represent time, which  
412: agents can learn to do, but where the evidence it relies on resides. We call a record irreversible for 
413: the agent  if, under the declared checkpoint scope and threat model, neither the agent’s own  
414: operations nor a restoration of its state can undo it; the term denotes non-restorability relative to 
415: those declarations, not irreversibility in an absolute sense. Only authenticated evidence outside  
416: the restorable state — held by trusted hardware or produced by a physical process — can be  
417: irreversible in this sense, and only under a threat model in which that source and its readout  
418: remain trustworthy.
419: Four properties matter, and physics is one route to them, not the argument for them: autonomy — 
420: the source evolves without being written by the program;  non-restorability — no checkpoint  
421: restores it; coupling — it is read by the computation, ideally by the self-model; and uniqueness — 
422: it is not copied with the agent. Proposals for mortal computation give up hardware independence 
423: and tie what is learned to a particular physical substrate [22, 41]. The motive here is evidence  
424: rather than efficiency, but the cost is shared: part of the agent can no longer be copied or replayed.
425: Biology offers no restorable state, and its timing mechanisms are instructive as analogies and  
426: candidate mechanisms, not as evidence that artificial agents need them. The brain has no single  
427: central clock: time can be encoded in the evolving state of neural networks [26]; a slowly drifting 
428: temporal context supports memory for when things happened [23]; population activity in the  
429: lateral entorhinal cortex carries time derived from the flow of experience [65]; and the drift of  
430: hippocampal  ensembles  over  days  is  sufficient  to  timestamp  events  [58].  Drift  with  similar 
431: geometry appears in artificial networks trained continually with dropout [1]. The subjective sense 
432: of duration has been proposed to draw on the accumulation of bodily, interoceptive states [14,  
433: 
434: PAGE 8
435: A time of their own: agent-held temporal evidence
436: I. D. Ionici · Perspective, version 1.4 · [DATA PUBLICĂRII] November 2026 8
437: 69] — a debated hypothesis, but the closest biological analogue of a time generated by a system’s 
438: own physical processes rather than read from an external clock.
439: 4. A Continuity Anchor and a Dual Clock
440: Two roles must be kept apart: detecting discontinuities of the agent’s trajectory, and modelling  
441: how the agent changes along it. They need different quantities.
442: The  continuity  anchor. Detection  requires  a  non-restorable  time  reference  ca and  a  non-
443: restorable count N of the agent’s own committed steps, both read through the trusted path and  
444: bound to the state: the restored state carries sealed copies of the values at which it was saved,  
445: ca(t0) and N(t0). When a state is loaded, the live values are compared with the sealed copies it  
446: carries. If the live N exceeds N(t0), the agent has committed steps since that state was saved, so the 
447: loaded state is not its latest: it has been restored. If N equals N(t0) while ca has advanced beyond 
448: its calibration error, no step has been committed since the save: the agent was suspended, or ran  
449: without committing a step. While the agent runs, each commit renews the sealed copies, so both  
450: values advance together. Under the assumptions stated below — one lineage, a counter the host  
451: cannot reset and a key it cannot read, and crash-consistent commits — the pair (ca, N) suffices for 
452: this  distinction; forking  and  cloning,  discussed below, lie  outside  it. The  pair is  what  state-
453: continuity protocols already maintain for data [36, 40, 47, 61]. Such protocols remain necessary  
454: because, once power is off, the non-volatile state of a trusted execution environment can be  
455: reverted  undetectably  to  an  older  version  [27];  the  TPM  2.0  clock  already  separates  time 
456: references by the events that reset them, with distinct counts of resets and restarts [64]. The  
457: sealed reference values must be protected by a key held in trusted hardware, or their digest kept  
458: by the monitor, so that a host adversary cannot rewrite them before restoring the state, and  
459: commits must be crash-consistent, so that a crash between incrementing the counter and saving  
460: the state is not mistaken for a restoration [61].
461: Protocol sketch. The sufficiency of the pair rests on an ordering that state-continuity protocols  
462: already provide; we sketch it only to make the assumptions explicit, not as a new protocol. (1) At  
463: each commit the agent proposes a state Sk+1; the monitor increments N to Nk+1, reads ca, and seals 
464: the tuple of the state digest H(Sk+1), Nk+1, ca, and a lineage identifier with a key that never leaves  
465: trusted hardware. (2) The state and its seal are committed together and crash-consistently, so  
466: that a crash leaves either the old pair or the new one. (3) When a state is loaded, the monitor  
467: verifies the seal, reads the live values of N and ca through the trusted path, and classifies the event: 
468: an equal counter with the time reference advanced beyond calibration error is a suspension, or  
469: an interval without committed steps, a larger counter is a restoration, and anything inconsistent  
470: with the seal is a forgery. (4) The classification is passed on as an adjudicated claim, or the raw  
471: readings as agent-bound evidence. Under threat model B the sketch is sufficient if the host cannot 
472: read the sealing key, cannot reorder steps (1) and (2), and cannot interpose between the monitor 
473: and the self-model; races between checking and using a reading, and threat model C, lie outside it.
474: Copying differs from restoration. A fork of the same checkpoint on the same machine shares the  
475: time reference and the device-unique responses, and is revealed only when the branches contend 
476: for the same counter: once one branch commits, the other finds N ahead of its own record. A clone 
477: moved to another device can detect the change of substrate if its state carries a commitment to  
478: the original device’s unique responses [45], but the instance continuing on the original device sees 
479: 
480: PAGE 9
481: A time of their own: agent-held temporal evidence
482: I. D. Ionici · Perspective, version 1.4 · [DATA PUBLICĂRII] November 2026 9
483: no change. Neither case reveals which branch is the original or the legitimate successor; only a  
484: continuity policy outside the agent can decide that. Device-unique responses are themselves  
485: exposed  to  machine-learning  modelling  attacks  [59]  and  to  hardware  Trojans  that  remain 
486: dormant through validation [35], so monotonic counters and attestation in trusted execution  
487: environments are the more conservative choice in practice, and unclonable functions a candidate 
488: to be tested. Legitimate migration changes the substrate on purpose. It is best represented as an  
489: explicit re-anchoring event, authorised by the operator and sealed by the monitor, that binds the  
490: old anchor, the new anchor, the state digest, the counter, and the authority under which the move 
491: was  made;  the  self-model  can  then  treat  the  change  as  an  authorised  discontinuity  with 
492: documented continuity rather than as a clandestine copy — the role that fenced migration plays  
493: in architectures for runtime-independent agents [73].
494: The dual clock.  The second role — modelling the agent’s own adaptation — relates the time  
495: reference  to  an  experiential  trajectory  index  ce,  defined  as  accumulated  change  along  the 
496: trajectory, ce(t) = Σ τ<t D(S(τ), S(τ+1)) over committed steps. The index is non-decreasing and is  
497: maintained as one running number; a query about an interval needs only the value stored at its  
498: start. It is called a clock only by analogy: the persistent state itself drifts non-monotonically and  
499: can return near earlier regions, which is why the index accumulates change rather than measuring 
500: distance from a reference state. The net displacement D(S(t0), S(t1)) is a separate quantity, which 
501: can be small even when much has changed — when an update is learned and later revoked, or  
502: when a state cycles. The choice of D is itself an open problem: a distance between weights is not a 
503: difference in function. Candidates range from cheap and weak to costly and valid — the norm of  
504: parameter change, representational distance on a fixed set of probes, divergence of the action  
505: distribution on those probes, change in measured competences, and the estimated counterfactual 
506: influence of an experience. The cheapest candidate has already been used: the cumulative norm  
507: of parameter updates serves as a model-centric clock for scheduling replay in continual learning  
508: of language models [18]. That work compares the clock with step counts only. A second study  
509: asked whether such an index carries information beyond logged optimizer statistics [24]. Three  
510: parameter-space distances, in a synthetic continual-learning world, and the update-norm clock,  
511: in a 0.6-billion-parameter language model trained with LoRA and Adam, were reconstructed out  
512: of sample chiefly from the learning rate times the norm of Adam’s first moment, which the  
513: optimizer already holds; a functional distance on probes, in both settings, was reconstructed from 
514: the change of loss on the same probes, a competitor of the same cost (R² ≥ 0.90, except when a  
515: schedule family was left out of training). Under Adam the size of a step is set by moments the  
516: optimizer keeps, so this was expected for the parameter-space quantities. Used as a replay clock,  
517: the update-norm index did no better than that free quantity in the regimes tested: in a re-
518: implementation, where the two clocks triggered replay at practically the same steps and, on  
519: synthetic facts, a plain step count did as well, and in aggregate with the original code, where the  
520: free clock triggered replay systematically later. For these candidates, in these regimes, the index  
521: is therefore not identifiable: the study found no basis to test whether it predicts adaptation, and  
522: it does not show that it cannot. What remains open are the costly candidates — change in  
523: measured competences and counterfactual influence — and larger models, full fine-tuning, and  
524: other optimizers. The second study concerns state that changes through gradient updates of  
525: weights. Many persistent agents change mainly through writes to a memory outside their weights 
526: — records or documents retrieved when needed — or through the state of their tools, where no  
527: optimizer keeps a record. For them, an index accumulated over the persistent state, writes and  
528: 
529: PAGE 10
530: A time of their own: agent-held temporal evidence
531: I. D. Ionici · Perspective, version 1.4 · [DATA PUBLICĂRII] November 2026 10
532: revocations included, is untested; its natural competitors are what a memory system already  
533: records — the count and size of writes, versions, provenance, and revocations — and the same  
534: reconstruction may recur there. A candidate is admissible only if it is not reconstructed out of  
535: sample from its competitor’s inputs — the optimizer’s own state (learning rate, moment norms,  
536: schedule) or a measure of the same cost — and beats decoders restricted to within-episode update 
537: statistics, survives the reset criterion and randomisation of episode order where it is meant to  
538: reflect history, predicts a later functional consequence — a change in competence or the effect of  
539: an update — better than event counts and logged update summaries, and is used by the agent  
540: rather than merely decodable from it.
541: Read against the time reference, ρ = Δ ce/Δca is the rate at which the agent changes, and the ratio  
542: of net displacement to accumulated change measures how directed that change has been. A  
543: related internal rate, learning progress — the decrease of prediction error over a window —  
544: already drives curiosity-based exploration in developmental robotics [44]; it is measured against 
545: the agent’s own restorable count of steps, not against a non-restorable reference. Humans judge  
546: durations differently in retrospect, from what the interval left in memory, and in prospect, from  
547: attention to time [6], and a model that estimates duration from accumulated change in network  
548: activity reproduces human biases [57]. These quantities are inputs to anticipation and to the  
549: detection of anomalous change, such as an unusually rapid shift after a compromised update. They 
550: play no part in detecting suspension or restoration: an agent executing with frozen weights and  
551: no writes to its memory, with Δ N > 0 and Δ ce ≈ 0, is operating, not interrupted, and a restored  
552: agent fed synthetic experience before it decides can show normal change while only the anchor  
553: reveals the restoration. Knowledge age, finally, is a question for  ca, not  ce: what matters for  
554: staleness is how far the world has moved, not how much the agent has experienced.
555: Engineering limits.  Remanence-decay clocks are illustrative candidates for bounded, power-
556: cycle-dependent intervals, not proposed as lifetime clocks. They measure only intervals during  
557: which their cells are unpowered, from seconds to hours depending on the hardware, and are  
558: sensitive to temperature and to chip-to-chip variation [55]. They do not measure a suspension  
559: during  which  the  host  stays  powered,  they  saturate  long  before  knowledge  age  becomes 
560: interesting, and re-arming a cell must itself be controlled by trusted hardware, or a host adversary 
561: could reset it. A long-horizon reference under threat model B therefore needs a continuously  
562: powered timer inside the trusted boundary — and trusted time in such environments is itself  
563: exposed  to  manipulation  by  the  host,  which  has  required  dedicated  protocols  [5]  —  or  an 
564: irreversible degradation process, or staggered decay cells re-armed only by trusted hardware.  
565: Sequential-computation proofs certify a lower bound on the work done since a fresh challenge,  
566: not elapsed time [7]; physical time-lock puzzles, which tie the delay to the evaluation latency of  
567: noisy unclonable functions [60], are a further candidate whose physical anchoring brings the risks 
568: noted above. And an index read from a learning state inherits every confound of that state, which 
569: is where the cautions of §6 apply.
570: 5. Tests and Their Competitors
571: The hypothesis is testable only against competent conventional alternatives. Table 2 orders the  
572: tests from the most basic to the most integrative, credits the mechanisms on which each builds,  
573: and names the conventional competitor that could make it unnecessary. The order is not a chain  
574: 
575: PAGE 11
576: A time of their own: agent-held temporal evidence
577: I. D. Ionici · Perspective, version 1.4 · [DATA PUBLICĂRII] November 2026 11
578: of presuppositions: each level can be closed independently, and if an earlier level is closed, later  
579: levels proceed with the winning conventional mechanism substituted for the one that lost. Closing 
580: a physical implementation does not close the corresponding functional question. A level is closed 
581: — and the corresponding part of the hypothesis abandoned for that regime — if its competitor is 
582: non-inferior on the level’s primary metric within a margin fixed in advance, the smallest difference 
583: that would matter [30], and is no worse on any cost dimension declared in advance: hardware,  
584: energy, latency, storage, communication, recomputation, error rates, availability, robustness to  
585: attack,  and  administrative  complexity.  A  result  in  which  the  competitor  is  better  on  some 
586: dimensions and worse on others is reported as unresolved, with the trade-off stated; it neither  
587: closes  the  level  nor  counts  as  support  for  the  hypothesis.  A  test  can  also  end  before  any 
588: comparison  on  the  primary  metric.  If  a  candidate  is  reconstructed  out  of  sample  from  the 
589: competitor’s own inputs, above a threshold fixed in advance, it carries no information beyond  
590: them that the test could detect. The hypothesis is then reported as not identifiable for that  
591: candidate and regime: never as equivalence, never as support, and not as closure of the level. The 
592: candidate is set aside, and costlier candidates remain open. The exit concerns only the comparison 
593: that could not be run; a secondary comparison that was run, such as the use of a candidate as a  
594: replay clock, is reported by the rule above. This exit was added in version 1.4, after the second  
595: study met it [24], and applies to later tests; that study had fixed the same gate and the same  
596: outcome in its own protocol before its runs. Each level also declares a primary metric: calibration 
597: error and energy over the measured interval for Level 1; predictive validity per unit of storage for 
598: Level 2; recomputation for Level 3; detection latency and false-alarm rate for Level 4; decision  
599: regret for Level 5; and prediction error for Level 6.
600: These rules become a test only through an evaluation contract for each level, fixed before any data 
601: are collected: the benchmark environment, the threat model, the competitor, the primary metric, 
602: the declared cost dimensions, the margin, the planned sample size or simulation budget, and the  
603: statistical decision procedure. No such contract is provided here. The first to write is the contract 
604: for Level 4: detection of suspension and restoration against the flag of a trusted monitor, with the 
605: margin,  the  sample  of  events,  and  the  decision  rule  fixed  in  advance.  This  article  proposes 
606: falsifiable questions and candidate metrics, not complete protocols for falsifying them.

754: Level 2 has now been tested for its cheapest candidates: they are not identifiable in the regimes  
755: tested, which neither closes the level nor supports it [24] (§4, §5).
756: Level 4 is where the proposal is most distinctive and most exposed. The strongest competitor is  
757: local: a trusted monitor on the same host that evaluates the continuity predicate when a state is  
758: loaded and passes an authenticated flag. It shares the root of trust and the readout path of raw  
759: agent-bound evidence and avoids the calibration error of decay clocks, so equivalence, or an  
760: advantage for the flag, is the expected result for detection itself. Equivalence with a complete flag 
761: therefore serves as the competence audit of the comparison, and the level is closed for detection  
762: if the flag is non-inferior to the agent at no greater cost, by the rule above, across the regimes the  
763: tests must vary — a flag that is delayed, incomplete, or absent, as on a disconnected device or  
764: under an orchestrator that withholds it. What would then remain is whether graded, raw evidence 
765: improves the agent’s downstream self-predictions beyond a binary flag, which Levels 5 and 6 test. 
766: Recent systems resolve the side-effects of rollback and the reconciliation of branches entirely  
767: within the platform — by fencing irreversible tool effects across checkpoint restoration [74] and  
768: 
769: PAGE 13
770: A time of their own: agent-held temporal evidence
771: I. D. Ionici · Perspective, version 1.4 · [DATA PUBLICĂRII] November 2026 13
772: by operating-system primitives for forking and committing exploratory branches [67] — and they 
773: belong to the competitor.
774: Example tasks for Levels 4–6.  Four tasks make the later levels concrete; each compares raw  
775: agent-bound evidence with an adjudicated flag, with timestamps and logs, and with no evidence  
776: at all. ( i)  Re-verification of stale knowledge : the agent holds facts that change in the world at  
777: different rates and, after an interval, must decide which to use and which to re-verify; the metrics 
778: are decision regret, the rates of needless re-verification and of stale use, the cost of verification,  
779: and calibration. (ii) Revocation of a compromised experience: an episode is declared compromised 
780: after it has changed the persistent state, and the agent must decide what to restore, replay, and  
781: re-evaluate; the metrics are residual influence, distance to an agent that never saw the episode,  
782: collateral loss, recomputation, and false revocations, against full reset and provenance-guided  
783: selective replay [71]. (iii) Prospective update: before accepting an update, the agent predicts which 
784: competences it will change, how strongly, and for how long; the metrics are prediction error, Brier 
785: scores for the risks predicted, and damage to protected competences. ( iv)  Continuity under a  
786: delayed flag: the agent operates after a restoration before an external verdict arrives; the metrics 
787: are detection latency, wrong decisions before detection, cumulative regret, and false alarms.  
788: Evaluation contracts that score state mutation and recovery obligations rather than answers [15], 
789: and the failure modes of checkpoint restoration documented in deployed agent systems [70],  
790: supply adversarial scenarios for all four.
791: 6. Method and Cautions from Two Completed Studies
792: The accompanying study asked a narrow question — whether binding an episode record to a  
793: summary of the internal change the episode produced helps a model attribute the episode’s  
794: source — in an offline, synthetic setting whose predictor never drives behaviour [25]. It does not 
795: test the programme: physical timing, continuity detection, delayed flags, closed-loop decisions,  
796: and self-prediction are absent from its data. What carries over are its gates, defined here so that  
797: they can be used without that study; each level of Table 2 still needs its own evaluation contract  
798: (§5), with controls specific to its threat model. An information-matched control receives the same 
799: evidence in another form, so that a comparison tests how the evidence is represented, not how  
800: much of it is available. The  decoding ceiling  of a level is the performance of an independent  
801: decoder, trained on substantially more data than the agents, in predicting the level’s target from  
802: the evidence available; it estimates what the evidence supports. A control passes the competence 
803: audit if it reaches a prespecified fraction of that ceiling — one half in the accompanying study —  
804: and exceeds a version of itself deprived of the evidence by a prespecified margin. Equivalence is 
805: declared when the confidence interval of a difference lies within a prespecified margin, by two  
806: one-sided tests [30].  Onset versus skill  reports separately whether, and when, each model first  
807: exceeds a prespecified minimum skill, and the skill both reach once both have exceeded it, so that 
808: a difference in whether learning begins is not mistaken for a difference in what is learned.  
809: Construct-validity diagnostics retrain the decoder on reduced inputs to check what actually carries 
810: the signal. Two results of the study are cautions for the present proposal.
811: • An apparent advantage can be an artefact of an incompetent control.  In a three-class  
812: source-attribution task, explicit binding appeared to win by 0.38 in Brier skill score against 
813: generic controls; once the control could use the information it was given, the difference  
814: 
815: PAGE 14
816: A time of their own: agent-held temporal evidence
817: I. D. Ionici · Perspective, version 1.4 · [DATA PUBLICĂRII] November 2026 14
818: vanished. Any advantage of agent-held evidence must survive a competent conventional  
819: competitor.
820: • Local statistics can stand in for history.  The source of an episode was carried by the  
821: magnitude and variability of the updates within it, and resetting the state before every  
822: episode did not reduce decodability. Any claim that an agent uses its accumulated history  
823: must pass a reset criterion, because shortcuts available in the inputs will be found [19].
824: The second study [24] adds two cautions.
825: • A model-centred quantity can be the optimizer’s bookkeeping. Under Adam the size of 
826: every  step  is  set  by  moments  the  optimizer  already  keeps.  In  a  language  model,  the 
827: accumulated norm of parameter change was reconstructed from the learning rate times the 
828: norm of the first moment with held-out R² 0.94–0.96 (0.96–0.97 with the summed learning 
829: rate added), and a functional distance on probes was reconstructed from the change of loss 
830: on the same probes. Before its predictive value is tested, any candidate index must be  
831: checked for reconstruction from its competitors’ inputs — free telemetry, or measures of  
832: the same cost: if it is reconstructed above a threshold fixed in advance, its value cannot be  
833: identified. The threshold is operational; the remainder it leaves untested could still matter.
834: • An  exception  found  by  extrapolation  is  a  limit  on  transfer,  not  yet  evidence  of 
835: information. A functional distance seemed to escape reconstruction in one schedule family, 
836: with that family left out of training and one seed (R² 0.81); with the family sampled, on eight 
837: new seeds and leaving one seed out, it was reconstructed at 0.99. The reconstruction has to 
838: be learned within the regime; an exception counts as evidence only if it persists there.
839: Physical components  add a  caution  of  their own: an  advantage of a physical  clock must be 
840: compared with a matched informational simulation of the same signal. Such a simulation controls 
841: for the statistics of the signal in non-adversarial tasks, under threat model A. Under threat model  
842: B what matters is only where the source resides relative to the checkpoint: a simulation inside the 
843: checkpoint is restored with it, and one outside it is a conventional trusted timer. Irreversibility  
844: counts only through the evidence it provides.
845: 7. Risks and a Design Principle
846: Detecting one’s own suspension, restoration, or copying is a form of situational awareness, which 
847: safety research measures because it can undermine evaluations [4, 29]; current models already  
848: distinguish  evaluation  from  deployment  transcripts  well  above  chance  [39].  An  indicator  of 
849: branching or rollback is also an indicator of being evaluated, since evaluations routinely branch  
850: and reset checkpoints. Rollback is, moreover, a legitimate instrument of governance: protocols  
851: that permit authorised rollback while preventing unauthorised rollback have been proposed  
852: precisely because recovery from corruption requires it [9], version control of agent memory lets  
853: users roll it back on request in natural language [62], and the difficulty of restoring a self-
854: modifying agent’s behaviour by reverting its self-description is itself a governance problem [63].
855: We therefore propose one design principle: access to evidence of restoration confers no control  
856: over it. The evidence is read-only to the agent; the agent has no write access to the mechanism and 
857: no authority over the orchestrator that restores it, and operators can restore it whatever it  
858: concludes. The principle is the evidential counterpart of safe interruptibility and corrigibility,  
859: 
860: PAGE 15
861: A time of their own: agent-held temporal evidence
862: I. D. Ionici · Perspective, version 1.4 · [DATA PUBLICĂRII] November 2026 15
863: where an agent must learn neither to prevent nor to seek interruption [42], interruptions it can  
864: detect are pruned from what it learns [16], and an incentive to observe a variable is formally  
865: distinct from an incentive to control it [17]. Those results concern interventions to come; here the 
866: intervention has already happened, and the question is what the agent may do with evidence of it. 
867: Three kinds of access are distinguished: bounded epistemic access, in which the evidence informs 
868: uncertainty,  estimates  of  staleness,  invalidation  of  cached  knowledge,  and  requests  for 
869: verification;  policy  access,  in  which  it  may  influence  external  actions  directly;  and  lifecycle 
870: authority, the power to block, authorise, or alter restoration. The first is the default proposed here; 
871: the  second  is  a  separate  capability  and  safety  decision  that  requires  mediation,  audit,  and 
872: revocable authorisation; the third is never granted to the agent. Privilege separation does not,  
873: however, make behaviour restorable by itself. By the proposition of §3, a restored agent that reads 
874: non-restorable evidence receives inputs different from those it received when its state was saved; 
875: if its policy conditions on them — by refusing work after a detected rollback, for instance —  
876: restoration reproduces the refusal rather than the earlier behaviour, and repeated restoration  
877: only widens the gap. Restoring behaviour therefore requires that discontinuity evidence enter  
878: only epistemic functions — estimates of staleness, invalidation of cached knowledge, calibrated  
879: uncertainty — rather than unconstrained action selection — readability by the self-model and  
880: readability by the whole policy are not the same thing — or that the platform virtualise the  
881: continuity evidence during governance recovery and evaluation. Virtualisation must itself be an  
882: authorised and audited operation, enforced and recorded by the trusted monitor rather than by  
883: the host [9], so that a compromised orchestrator cannot invoke it to mask a real discontinuity; it  
884: trades the protection the evidence offers under threat model B for operator control, and the trade 
885: should be explicit. Capabilities of this kind should be studied in contained settings, kept visible to 
886: overseers on the same terms as to the agent, and reported as dual-use.
887: Temporal  self-models  also  touch  properties  discussed  in  the  scientific  assessment  of 
888: consciousness in AI [11] and in the debate on AI welfare [32, 37], and research near them should  
889: follow principles for responsible conduct [10]. The proposal makes no claim in this direction, and 
890: the  evidence  counsels  against  one.  Temporal  continuity  and  consciousness  dissociate:  a 
891: professional musician with dense amnesia after encephalitis remained conscious while losing the 
892: continuity of experience [68]. The human capacity closest to the present target is autonoetic  
893: consciousness — awareness of oneself in subjective time [66] — and only its functional analogues 
894: are at issue here; the leading theories of consciousness, moreover, remain contested even under  
895: preregistered adversarial tests [13].
896: 8. Conclusion
897: Biological  organisms  develop  along  trajectories  that  cannot  be  restored  from  a  complete 
898: checkpoint, whereas artificial agents are commonly engineered so that their computational state  
899: can be copied and reinstated. The difference is not that one exists in time and the other does not,  
900: but that in the latter, evidence of elapsed time and of change can be erased together with the saved 
901: state. The mechanisms that could preserve such evidence already exist — in hardware security,  
902: in cryptography, and in the neuroscience of timing — but they serve the platform and the guard  
903: checks of protected modules, not the agent’s model of itself. We have proposed a classification of  
904: the evidence an agent can hold, an elementary result that locates the problem, a separation  
905: between a continuity anchor that detects discontinuities and a dual clock that serves the agent’s  
906: 
907: PAGE 16
908: A time of their own: agent-held temporal evidence
909: I. D. Ionici · Perspective, version 1.4 · [DATA PUBLICĂRII] November 2026 16
910: model of its own change, a sequence of tests with the competitors that could close them, and a  
911: principle that separates access to evidence from control over restoration. One level has since been 
912: tested for its cheapest candidates, which proved not identifiable: free optimizer telemetry, or a  
913: measure of the same cost, reconstructed each of them [24]; the accompanying exploratory study, 
914: which tests no level, had pointed the same way. The other tests, including those of the continuity  
915: anchor, where the proposal is most distinctive, are offered to others. Whether a time of their own 
916: makes persistent agents more capable, or only more complicated, is an empirical question, and  
917: the elements of a method for answering it are in place.
